\mathrm{dist}^{2}_{(0)}\lesssim\frac{\phi^{2/3}\alpha_{\mathrm{RSC}}}{\kappa^{2}\beta_{\mathrm{RSS}}}.

Proof of Proposition
6
.

By Proposition
5
, we know that with probability at least
1-C\exp\left(-C(p_{1}r_{1}+p_{2}r_{2})\right)
,

\mathrm{dist}^{2}_{(0)}\lesssim\phi^{2/3}\underline{\sigma}^{-4}g_{\min}^{-4}\alpha_{\mathrm{RSC}}^{-2}\tau^{2}M_{1}^{2}\frac{\mathrm{df}_{\mathrm{RRMAR}}}{T}.

Thus when
T\gtrsim g_{\min}^{-4}\kappa^{2}\underline{\sigma}^{-4}\alpha_{\mathrm{RSC}}^{-3}\beta_{\mathrm{RSS}}\tau^{2}M_{1}^{2}(p_{1}r_{1}+p_{2}r_{2})\gtrsim g_{\min}^{-4}\kappa^{2}\underline{\sigma}^{-4}\alpha_{\mathrm{RSC}}^{-3}\beta_{\mathrm{RSS}}\tau^{2}M_{1}^{2}\mathrm{df}_{\mathrm{RRMAR}}
, we have
\mathrm{dist}^{2}_{(0)}\leq C_{D}\kappa^{-2}\phi^{2/3}\alpha_{\mathrm{RSC}}\beta_{\mathrm{RSS}}^{-1}
.
∎

D.5
Auxiliary Lemmas

The first lemma is Hanson–Wright inequality and can be found in high-dimentional statistics monograph
(
Wainwright,, 2019
)
.

Lemma D.5
.

(
Wainwright,, 2019
)

Given random variables
\left\{X_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
and a positive semidefinite matrix
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{S}_{+}^{n\times n}
, consider the random quadratic form

Z=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbf{Q}_{ij}X_{i}X_{j}.

If the random variables
\left\{X_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
are i.i.d. with mean zero, unit variance, and
\sigma
-sub-Gaussian, then there are universal constants
\left(c_{1},c_{2}\right)
such that

\mathbb{P}[|Z-\mathbb{E}Z|\geq\sigma t]\leq 2\exp\left\{-\min\left(\frac{c_{1}t}{\|\mathbf{Q}\|_{2}},\frac{c_{2}t^{2}}{\|\mathbf{Q}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}\right)\right\},

where
\|\mathbf{Q}\|_{2}
and
\|\mathbf{Q}\|_{\mathrm{F}}
denote the operator nd Frobenius norms, respectively.

The second lemma establishs the covering number of
\mathcal{S}(r_{1},r_{2},p_{1},p_{2})
introduced in the proof of Proposition
2
.

Lemma D.6
.

Define the set of unit-norm differences of low-rank Kronecker products as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(r_{1},r_{2},p_{1},p_{2}):=\Big\{\mathbf{M}:

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{A}_{2}\otimes\mathbf{A}_{1}-\mathbf{A}_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{A}_{1}^{\prime},

(116)

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{A}_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{i}\times p_{i}},~\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{A}_{i}),~\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{A}_{i}^{\prime})\leq r_{i},~\left\|\mathbf{M}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}=1,~i=1,2\Big\}.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{S}}(r_{1},r_{2},p_{1},p_{2})
be an
\varepsilon
-net of
\mathcal{S}(r_{1},r_{2},p_{1},p_{2})
with respect to the Frobenius norm, where
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Then,

\left|\overline{\mathcal{S}}(r_{1},r_{2},p_{1},p_{2})\right|\leq\left(\frac{15}{\varepsilon}\right)^{4p_{1}r_{1}+4p_{2}r_{2}+16r_{1}^{2}r_{2}^{2}}.

(117)

Proof of Lemma
D.6
.

For brevity, given
(r_{1},r_{2},p_{1},p_{2})
, we use
\mathcal{S}
and
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
to represent
\mathcal{S}(r_{1},r_{2},p_{1},p_{2})
and
\overline{\mathcal{S}}(r_{1},r_{2},p_{1},p_{2})
, respectively. Since the proof utilizes tensor operations as techniques, we give some basic notations and tensor algebra relevant to the proof for clearity. We use 4-dimensional tensors to illustrate the concepts.

Our definitions align with
Kolda and Bader, (2009)
. For any tensor
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}\times d_{3}\times d_{4}}
, denote its
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})
-th entry as
\mathcal{X}_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}}
. The Frobenius norm is defined by

\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\mathrm{F}}=\sqrt{\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{d_{1}}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{d_{2}}\sum_{i_{3}=1}^{d_{3}}\sum_{i_{4}=1}^{d_{4}}\mathcal{X}_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}}^{2}}.

(118)

The mode-
k
matricization of a tensor
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}
, denoted by
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{(k)}
, is a
d_{k}\times(d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}d_{4}/d_{k})
matrix. Specifically,
\mathcal{X}_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}}
maps to matrix element
\left(i_{k},j\right)
, where

j=1+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n=1\\
n\neq k\end{subarray}}^{4}\left(i_{n}-1\right)J_{n}\quad\text{ with }\quad J_{n}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}m=1\\
m\neq k\end{subarray}}^{n-1}d_{m}.

For a matrix
\mathbf{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times d_{1}}
, the 1-mode product, denoted by
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}\times_{1}\mathbf{U}
, is of size
p_{1}\times d_{2}\times d_{3}\times d_{4}
. It is defined by

(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}\times_{1}\mathbf{U})_{i^{\prime},j,k,l}=\sum_{i=1}^{d_{1}}\mathbf{U}_{i^{\prime},i}\mathcal{X}_{i,j,k,l},

The 2,3 and 4-mode products are defined analogously. Let
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Y}}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}\times_{1}\mathbf{U}_{1}\times_{2}\mathbf{U}_{2}\times_{3}\mathbf{U}_{3}\times_{4}\mathbf{U}_{4}
with
\mathbf{U}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{k}\times d_{k}}
, its
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
-th entry can be written as

\mathcal{Y}_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k^{\prime},l^{\prime}}=\sum_{i=1}^{d_{1}}\sum_{j=2}^{d_{2}}\sum_{k=3}^{d_{3}}\sum_{l=4}^{d_{4}}\mathcal{X}_{i,j,k,l}(\mathbf{U}_{1})_{i^{\prime},i}(\mathbf{U}_{2})_{j^{\prime},j}(\mathbf{U}_{3})_{k^{\prime},k}(\mathbf{U}_{4})_{l^{\prime},l}.

(119)

An important property is that

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Y}}_{(k)}=\mathbf{U}_{k}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{(k)}\left(\mathbf{U}_{4}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathbf{U}_{(k+1)}\otimes\mathbf{U}_{(k-1)}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathbf{U}_{1}\right)^{\top}.

(120)

Next, we introduce the concept of Tucker rank. Defining
r_{k}=\text{rank}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{(k)})
for
k=1,2,3,4
, the Tucker rank of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}
is given by
(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},r_{4})
. If
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}
is low-Tucker-rank, i.e.,
r_{k}\leq\bar{r}_{k}
for some
\bar{r}_{k}
and
k=1,2,3,4
, it can be decomposed as

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}\times_{1}\mathbf{U}_{1}\times_{2}\mathbf{U}_{2}\times_{3}\mathbf{U}_{3}\times_{4}\mathbf{U}_{4},

(121)

where
\mathbf{U}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}\times\bar{r}_{k}}
having orthonormal columns and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\bar{r}_{1}\times\bar{r}_{2}\times\bar{r}_{3}\times\bar{r}_{4}}
. The decomposition can be obtained by HOSVD, see
De Lathauwer et al., (2000)
for more details.

Now we start our proof. We first validate that Kronecker products can be viewed as 4-dimensional tensors. For any matrix
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{2}p_{1}\times p_{2}p_{1}}
, define the rearrangement operator
\mathcal{T}:\mathbb{R}^{p_{2}p_{1}\times p_{2}p_{1}}\to\mathbb{R}^{p_{2}\times p_{1}\times p_{2}\times p_{1}}
. The operation is defined element-wise as follows: if
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{M}_{2}\otimes\mathbf{M}_{1}
where
\mathbf{M}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{i}\times p_{i}}
for
i=1,2
, then

\left[\mathcal{T}(\mathbf{M}_{2}\otimes\mathbf{M}_{1})\right]_{i_{1},j_{1},i_{2},j_{2}}=\left(\mathbf{M}_{2}\right)_{i_{1},i_{2}}\left(\mathbf{M}_{1}\right)_{j_{1},j_{2}},~\text{for all}~i_{1},i_{2}=1\ldots,p_{2},~\text{and}~j_{1},j_{2}=1,\ldots,p_{1}.

(122)

Since
\mathcal{T}
is a bijection, the inverse operator
\mathcal{T}^{-1}
is well-defined. Furthermore,
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}^{-1}
are linear isometry with respect to the Frobenius norm. Therefore, we find the
\varepsilon
-net of
\mathcal{S}
by applying
\mathcal{T}^{-1}
on the
\varepsilon
-net of
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{S}):=\{\mathcal{T}(\mathbf{M}):\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{S}\}
. Denote
\overline{\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{S})}
to be the
\varepsilon
-net
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{S})
with respect to the tensor Frobenius norm, we have
|\overline{\mathcal{S}}|=|\overline{\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{S})}|
.

It remains to characterize the structure of
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{S})
and bound the covering number of it. For any
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{S}
, it is decomposed as
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{A}_{2}\otimes\mathbf{A}_{1}-\mathbf{A}_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{A}_{1}^{\prime}
, where
\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{A}_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{i}\times p_{i}}
with
\text{rank}(\mathbf{A}_{i})
and
\text{rank}(\mathbf{A}_{i}^{\prime})\leq r_{i}
. Define their SVD components as
\mathbf{A}_{i}=\mathbf{U}_{i}\mathbf{S}_{i}\mathbf{V}_{i}^{\top}
and
\mathbf{A}_{i}^{\prime}=\mathbf{U}_{i}^{\prime}\mathbf{S}_{i}^{\prime}\mathbf{V}_{i}^{\prime\top}
, where
\mathbf{U}_{i}
,
\mathbf{U}_{i}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{V}_{i}
, and
\mathbf{V}_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{O}^{p_{i}\times r_{i}}
, for
i=1,2
. Denote
\sigma_{k}
and
\gamma_{k}
to be the
k
-th largest singular value of
\mathbf{A}_{2}
and
\mathbf{A}_{1}
, respectively. Applying
\mathcal{T}
on
\mathbf{A}_{2}\otimes\mathbf{A}_{1}
, the
(i_{1},j_{1},i_{2},j_{2})
-th entry is

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{T}(\mathbf{A}_{2}\otimes\mathbf{A}_{1})\right]_{i_{1},j_{1},i_{2},j_{2}}

(123)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{k=1}^{r_{2}}\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{U}_{2})_{i_{1},k}(\mathbf{V}_{2})_{i_{2},k}\right)\left(\sum_{l=1}^{r_{1}}\gamma_{l}(\mathbf{U}_{1})_{j_{1},l}(\mathbf{V}_{1})_{j_{2},l}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{r_{2}}\sum_{l=1}^{r_{1}}\sigma_{k}\gamma_{l}(\mathbf{U}_{2})_{i_{1},k}(\mathbf{V}_{2})_{i_{2},k}(\mathbf{U}_{1})_{j_{1},l}(\mathbf{V}_{1})_{j_{2},l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{r_{2}}\sum_{l=1}^{r_{1}}\sum_{m=1}^{r_{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{r_{1}}\sigma_{k}\gamma_{l}1_{\{m=k\}}1_{\{n=l\}}(\mathbf{U}_{2})_{i_{1},k}(\mathbf{V}_{2})_{i_{2},m}(\mathbf{U}_{1})_{j_{1},l}(\mathbf{V}_{1})_{j_{2},n},

where
1_{\{\cdot\}}
is the indicator function. It corresponds to the form of mode product given in (
119
), which implies that

\mathcal{T}(\mathbf{A}_{2}\otimes\mathbf{A}_{1})=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{C}}\times_{1}\mathbf{U}_{2}\times_{2}\mathbf{U}_{1}\times_{3}\mathbf{V}_{2}\times_{4}\mathbf{V}_{1},

(124)

where
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{2}\times r_{1}\times r_{2}\times r_{1}}
is a low-dimensional core tensor being defined by the last line of (
123
). Then, by matricization with (
120
) and checking the ranks of four modes, we know that
\mathcal{T}(\mathbf{A}_{2}\otimes\mathbf{A}_{1})
is low-Tucker-rank with ranks of four modes being at most
(r_{2},r_{1},r_{2},r_{1})
. Similar argument holds analogously for
\mathcal{T}(\mathbf{A}_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{A}_{1}^{\prime})
. Therefore, by the property of matrix ranks, the Tucker rank of
\mathcal{T}(\mathbf{M})=\mathcal{T}(\mathbf{A}_{2}\otimes\mathbf{A}_{1})-\mathcal{T}(\mathbf{A}_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{A}_{1}^{\prime})
are at most
(2r_{2},2r_{1},2r_{2},2r_{1})
. Applying Lemma
D.9
, we have that

|\overline{\mathcal{S}}|=|\overline{\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{S})}|\leq\left(\frac{15}{\varepsilon}\right)^{4p_{1}r_{1}+4p_{2}r_{2}+16r_{1}^{2}r_{2}^{2}}.

(125)

∎

The next lemma is some conclusions on splitting martices with common dimensions. Its goal is to show that we can decompose the sum of two martices equipped with common dimensions as the sum of four matrices. Each of them are prependicular to the others, two of them are low-rank without common dimensions and the other two are equipped with the same common dimensions.

Lemma D.7
.

Suppose that there are two
p\times p
rank-
r
matrices
\mathbf{W}_{1}
and
\mathbf{W}_{2}
. Then,

1.

There exists two
p\times p
rank-
r
matrices
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{1},\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{2}
, such that
\mathbf{W}_{1}+\mathbf{W}_{2}=\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{1}+\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{2}
and
\left\langle\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{1},\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{2}\right\rangle=0
.

2.

Suppose that now
\mathbf{W}_{1}
and
\mathbf{W}_{2}
both have common dimension
d
. That is,
Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\prime}(r,d;p)=\left\{\mathbf{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}:\mathbf{W}=[\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}]\mathbf{D}[\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}]^{\top},\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{O}^{p\times d},\mathbf{R},\mathbf{P}\in\mathbb{O}^{p\times(r-d)},\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left\langle\mathbf{C},\mathbf{R}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathbf{C},\mathbf{P}\right\rangle=0\right\}

to be the model space of MARCF model with arbitrary scale,

\mathbf{W}_{1},\mathbf{W}_{2}\in\mathcal{W}^{\prime}(r,d;p)
. Then there exist
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{1},\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{2}\in\mathcal{W}^{\prime}(r,d;p)
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{3},\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{4}\in\mathcal{W}^{\prime}(r,0;p)
, such that
\mathbf{W}_{1}+\mathbf{W}_{2}=\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{1}+\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{2}+\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{3}+\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{4}
and
\left\langle\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{j},\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{k}\right\rangle=0
for every
j,k=1,2,3,4,j\neq k
.

Proof of Lemma
D.7
.

For the first part of the lemma, by SVD decomposition we know that there exists
p\times r
matrices
\mathbf{U}_{1},\mathbf{U}_{2},\mathbf{V}_{1},\mathbf{V}_{2}
with mutually orthogonal columns, such that
\mathbf{U}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{1}=\mathbf{I}_{r_{1}},\mathbf{W}_{1}=\mathbf{U}_{1}\mathbf{V}_{1}^{\top}
and
\mathbf{W}_{2}=\mathbf{U}_{2}\mathbf{V}_{2}^{\top}
. Let

\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{1}=\mathbf{U}_{1}(\mathbf{V}_{1}^{\top}+\mathbf{U}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{2}\mathbf{V}_{2}^{\top})
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{2}=(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{U}_{1}\mathbf{U}_{1}^{\top})\mathbf{U}_{2}\mathbf{V}_{2}^{\top}
, then
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{1}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{2}
satisfy the conditions.

For the second part, decompose and write together
\mathbf{W}_{1}+\mathbf{W}_{2}
gives

\begin{aligned} \mathbf{W}_{1}+\mathbf{W}_{2}=[\mathbf{C}_{1}~\mathbf{R}_{1}~\mathbf{C}_{2}~\mathbf{R}_{2}]\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{D}_{1}&\mathbf{O}\\
\mathbf{O}&\mathbf{D}_{2}\end{bmatrix}[\mathbf{C}_{1}~\mathbf{P}_{1}~\mathbf{C}_{2}~\mathbf{P}_{2}]^{\top}\end{aligned}.

By applying Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization procedure (or equivalently, QR decomposition), we can obtain two sets of orthogonal basis,

[\mathbf{C}_{1}~\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{1}~\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{2}~\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{2}]\in\mathbb{O}^{p\times(r_{1}+r_{2})},\quad[\mathbf{C}_{1}~\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{1}~\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{2}~\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{2}]\in\mathbb{O}^{p\times(r_{1}+r_{2})}.

Then

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{1}+\mathbf{W}_{2}=

\displaystyle[\mathbf{C}_{1}~\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{1}~\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{2}~\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{2}]\begin{bmatrix}\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}_{1}&\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}_{3}\\
\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}_{4}&\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}_{2}\end{bmatrix}[\mathbf{C}_{1}~\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{1}~\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{2}~\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{2}]^{\top}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\mathbf{C}_{1}~\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{1}]\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}_{1}[\mathbf{C}_{1}~\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{1}]^{\top}+[\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{2}~\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{2}]\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}_{2}[\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{2}~\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{2}]^{\top}

\displaystyle+[\mathbf{C}_{1}~\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{1}]\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}_{3}[\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{2}~\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{2}]^{\top}+[\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{2}~\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{2}]\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}_{4}[\mathbf{C}_{1}~\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{1}]^{\top}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{1}+\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{2}+\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{3}+\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{4}.

It can be directly verified that
\left\langle\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{j},\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}_{k}\right\rangle=0
for every
j,k=1,2,3,4,j\neq k
.
∎

The next lemma gives the covering number of low rank martices with common column and row spaces.

Lemma D.8
.

(Lemma B.6,
Wang et al., (2023)
)
Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(r,d;p)=\left\{\mathbf{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}:\mathbf{W}=[\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}]\mathbf{D}[\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}]^{\top},\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{O}^{p\times d},\mathbf{R},\mathbf{P}\in\mathbb{O}^{p\times(r-d)},\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left\langle\mathbf{C},\mathbf{R}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathbf{C},\mathbf{P}\right\rangle=0,\textrm{ and }\|\mathbf{W}\|_{\mathrm{F}}=1\right\}.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{W}}(r,d;p)
be an
\epsilon
-net of
\mathcal{W}(r,d;p)
, where
\epsilon\in(0,1]
. Then

|\overline{\mathcal{W}}(r,d;p)|\leq\left(\frac{24}{\epsilon}\right)^{p(2r-d)+r^{2}}.

Finally, the following lemma establishes the covering number bound for the set of low-Tucker-rank tensors.

Lemma D.9
.

(Lemma 2,
Rauhut et al., (2017)
)
Define

\mathcal{H}=\left\{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}\times\cdots\times n_{N}}:\operatorname{rank}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{(k)})\leq r_{k},k=1,2,...,N,\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}\|_{\mathrm{F}}=1\right\}.

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, the covering number of
\mathcal{H}
with respect to the Frobenius norm satisfies:

\mathcal{N}\left(\mathcal{H},\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{F}},\varepsilon\right)\leq(3(N+1)/\varepsilon)^{r_{1}r_{2}\cdots r_{N}+\sum_{k=1}^{N}n_{k}r_{k}}.

Appendix E
Consistency of Rank Selection

Proof of Theorem
3
:
We focus on the consistency for selecting
r_{1}
, as the result for
r_{2}
can be developed analogously. With
T\gtrsim(p_{1}r_{1}+p_{2}r_{2}+4r_{1}^{2}r_{2}^{2})M_{2}^{-2}\max\left(\tau,\tau^{2}\right)
, by Proposition
5
,

\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{1}^{\mathrm{RR}}(\bar{r}_{1})-\mathbf{A}_{1}^{*}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}+\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{2}^{\mathrm{RR}}(\bar{r}_{2})-\mathbf{A}_{2}^{*}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\lesssim\phi^{-1}\alpha_{\mathrm{RSC}}^{-1}\tau M_{1}\sqrt{\frac{p_{1}+p_{2}}{T}}

with probability approaching
1
as
T\to\infty
and
p_{1},p_{2}\to\infty
. Since
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{1}^{\mathrm{RR}}-\mathbf{A}_{1}^{*}
is rank-
(\bar{r}_{1}+r_{1})
, by the fact that
L_{\infty}
norm is smaller than
L_{2}
norm and Mirsky’s singular value inequality
Mirsky, (1960)
,

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq j\leq\bar{r}_{1}+r_{1}}|\sigma_{j}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{1}^{\mathrm{RR}}(\bar{r}_{1}))-\sigma_{j}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{*})|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\bar{r}_{1}+r_{1}}\left(\sigma_{j}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{1}^{\mathrm{RR}}(\bar{r}_{1}))-\sigma_{j}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{*})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\bar{r}_{1}+r_{1}}\sigma^{2}_{j}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{1}^{\mathrm{RR}}(\bar{r}_{1})-\mathbf{A}_{1}^{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{1}^{\mathrm{RR}}(\bar{r}_{1})-\mathbf{A}_{1}^{*}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\phi^{-2}\alpha_{\mathrm{RSC}}^{-2}\tau^{2}M_{1}^{2}\frac{p_{1}+p_{2}}{T}.

Then,
\forall j=1,2,...,\bar{r}_{1}
,

|\sigma_{j}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{1}^{\mathrm{RR}}(\bar{r}_{1}))-\sigma_{j}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{*})|=O(\phi^{-1}\alpha_{\mathrm{RSC}}^{-1}\tau M_{1}\sqrt{(p_{1}+p_{2})/T}).

(126)

Next, we show that as
T,p_{1},p_{2}\to\infty
, the ratio
(\sigma_{j+1}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{1}^{\mathrm{RR}}(\bar{r}_{1}))+s(p_{1},p_{2},T))/(\sigma_{j}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{1}^{\mathrm{RR}}(\bar{r}_{1}))+s(p_{1},p_{2},T))
achieves its minimum at
j=r_{1}
. For
j>r_{1}
,
\sigma_{j}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{*})=0
and

\sigma_{j}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{1}^{\mathrm{RR}}(\bar{r}_{1}))=O(\phi^{-1}\alpha_{\mathrm{RSC}}^{-1}\tau M_{1}\sqrt{\mathrm{df}_{\mathrm{RRMAR}}/T})=o(s(p_{1},p_{2},T)).

(127)

Therefore, we have

\frac{\sigma_{j+1}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{1}^{\mathrm{RR}}(\bar{r}_{1}))+s(p_{1},p_{2},T)}{\sigma_{j}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{1}^{\mathrm{RR}}(\bar{r}_{1}))+s(p_{1},p_{2},T)}\to 1.

For
j<r_{1}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\to\infty\\
p_{1},p_{2}\to\infty\end{subarray}}\frac{\sigma_{j+1}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{1}^{\mathrm{RR}}(\bar{r}_{1}))+s(p_{1},p_{2},T)}{\sigma_{j}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{1}^{\mathrm{RR}}(\bar{r}_{1}))+s(p_{1},p_{2},T)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\to\infty\\
p_{1},p_{2}\to\infty\end{subarray}}\frac{\sigma_{j+1}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{*})+o(s(p_{1},p_{2},T))+s(p_{1},p_{2},T)}{\sigma_{j}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{*})+o(s(p_{1},p_{2},T))+s(p_{1},p_{2},T)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{p_{1},p_{2}\to\infty}\frac{\sigma_{j+1}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{*})}{\sigma_{j}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{*})}\leq 1.

For
j=r_{1}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{j+1}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{1}^{\mathrm{RR}}(\bar{r}_{1}))+s(p_{1},p_{2},T)}{\sigma_{j}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{1}^{\mathrm{RR}}(\bar{r}_{1}))+s(p_{1},p_{2},T)}=

\displaystyle\frac{o(s(p_{1},p_{2},T))+s(p_{1},p_{2},T)}{\sigma_{r_{1}}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{*})+o(s(p_{1},p_{2},T))+s(p_{1},p_{2},T)}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\frac{s(p_{1},p_{2},T)}{\sigma_{r_{1}}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{*})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o\left(\min_{1\leq j\leq r_{1}-1}\frac{\sigma_{j+1}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{*})}{\sigma_{j}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{*})}\right).

Therefore, when
T,p_{1},p_{2}\to\infty
, the ratio will finally achieve its minimum at
j=r_{1}
, with a probability approaching one.
